[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n2\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.0, F44.9, F32.9, F06.7\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s nice to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because my mind keeps… blanking out, I guess. Like at work, I’ll be looking right at the road-closure forms or holding the stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. It’s embarrassing, because that job is all about paying attention, and I used to know the routine like making coffee—well, I don’t even make it the same way every day, my sister says I put too much creamer in it—but with the road stuff, it’s serious. I just freeze and can’t think.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something very upsetting or frightening happened to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it did. I mean, I think that’s when it really started—after my closest friend died. That was about… a year and eight months ago now, which is strange because I still count it by months sometimes, like I’m keeping track of something. I’m not sure if it’s worry exactly. I mean, I don’t stand there thinking, “I’m worried,” it’s more like everything gets… quiet and jammed up. Then afterward I think about it a lot, like what if I missed something, or if someone noticed.

It’s embarrassing, because that job is all about paying attention, and I used to know the routine like making coffee—well, I don’t even make it the same way every day, my sister says I put too much creamer in it—but with the road stuff, it’s serious. I just freeze and can’t think.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something very upsetting or frightening happened to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it did. I mean, I think that’s when it really started—after my closest friend died. That was about… a year and eight months ago now, which is strange because I still count it by months sometimes, like I’m keeping track of something. We used to talk about the dumbest things, grocery store music and whether birds know when it’s raining before people do, and then after she was gone, everything kind of… shifted. So, yes, after that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times when you couldn’t remember important personal information about yourself, not just the steps of a task?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not like my name or where I live or anything like that, if that’s what you mean. I mean, sometimes I’ll walk into a room and forget why I’m there, but my sister says everybody does that, especially if you’re carrying laundry and thinking about the grocery list at the same time.\n\nImportant personal information… I guess I’m not sure what counts. I haven’t noticed big pieces of myself missing, no. It’s more like I get stuck on what I’m supposed to be doing right then.\n\nDoctor: When your mind goes blank at work, do you feel intensely worried right then?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure if it’s worry exactly. I mean, I don’t stand there thinking, “I’m worried,” it’s more like everything gets… quiet and jammed up. Then afterward I think about it a lot, like what if I missed something, or if someone noticed.\n\nBut right in the moment? I don’t know. It’s hard to separate, because the cars are there and the signs, and sometimes there’s that smell from the asphalt when it’s hot, and my gloves never fit right. So I know something is happening, but I don’t know if “intensely worried” is the right word.\n\nDoctor: When your mind goes blank, does the world around you ever feel unreal or dreamlike?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… dreamlike is a hard word for me. Sometimes when I’m standing there it feels like I’m not catching up with what’s happening, like the cars and people are moving and I’m a second behind, but I don’t know if that means unreal.\n\nIt’s not like in movies where everything goes wavy or anything. I mostly notice little things, like the pole feeling too heavy or the beeping from a truck backing up, and then I’m thinking about how those beeps sound like the microwave my mom had when I was a kid, which is… not helpful when I’m supposed to be watching traffic. So, I guess I haven’t really thought of it as the world feeling dreamlike.\n\nDoctor: When your mind goes blank, do memories or images of your friend’s death suddenly come into your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not exactly like that. I mean, I know what you’re asking, I think, like if a picture of it just pops in, but when my mind goes blank it’s more like there’s nothing there for a second.\n\nSometimes a random thing will remind me of other random things, like a sound or the way the light hits the road, and then I’m off thinking about something else. Like yesterday there was this crow sitting on the sign, and I started thinking about how smart crows are and how my neighbor used to feed them peanuts. But I haven’t really noticed memories or images of my friend’s death coming in right then, no.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yeah… most days, I think. Especially in the mornings. I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the day is already wrecked before I’ve even gotten out of bed.

Yeah… I have. I used to be the one doing things, you know? Like organizing rides for choir, and garden swaps—people would bring tomatoes and little pots of herbs, and someone always brought too much zucchini—and I’d help with snacks for the youth basketball games.

It’s embarrassing, because that job is all about paying attention, and I used to know the routine like making coffee—well, I don’t even make it the same way every day, my sister says I put too much creamer in it—but with the road stuff, it’s serious. I mean, I think that’s when it really started—after my closest friend died. So, yes, after that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times when you couldn’t remember important personal information about yourself, not just the steps of a task?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not like my name or where I live or anything like that, if that’s what you mean. I haven’t noticed big pieces of myself missing, no. It’s more like I get stuck on what I’m supposed to be doing right then.\n\nDoctor: When your mind goes blank at work, do you feel intensely worried right then?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure if it’s worry exactly. It’s hard to separate, because the cars are there and the signs, and sometimes there’s that smell from the asphalt when it’s hot, and my gloves never fit right. Sometimes when I’m standing there it feels like I’m not catching up with what’s happening, like the cars and people are moving and I’m a second behind, but I don’t know if that means unreal.\n\nIt’s not like in movies where everything goes wavy or anything. I mostly notice little things, like the pole feeling too heavy or the beeping from a truck backing up, and then I’m thinking about how those beeps sound like the microwave my mom had when I was a kid, which is… not helpful when I’m supposed to be watching traffic. not.\n- key: S020\n  name: Hallucinations\n  description: Perception-like experiences that occur without an external stimulus and are as vivid and clear as normal perception.\n  manifestation: When the house is quiet or I am standing near a work zone, I hear voices clear as day calling my name and saying I caused trouble on the block, but nobody else hears them.\n- key: S023\n  name: Negative_Symptoms\n  description: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.\n  manifestation: I used to be the one organizing choir rides, garden swaps, and youth basketball snacks, but now I can sit for hours staring at my phone and not feel the push to answer anybody.\n- key: S024\n  name: Depressed_Mood\n  description: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.\n  manifestation: Most mornings I wake up with this heavy, hollow sadness in my chest, like the day is already ruined before I even put my feet on the floor.\n- key: S026\n  name: Weight_Appetite_Change\n  description: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.\n  manifestation: Food has stopped meaning much to me, and I skip meals without noticing until my flagger pants are loose and my family starts asking why I look so drawn.\n- key: S028\n  name: Psychomotor_Changes\n  description: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.\n  manifestation: It takes me so long to bathe, dress, and get to the door that my family says I move like I am underwater, though sometimes I end up pacing the hallway wringing my hands because I cannot settle.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: I keep replaying every little thing I failed to do for my friend, the choir, and my own family, and I feel like I am a useless person who deserves whatever punishment is coming.\n- key: S031\n  name: Diminished_Thinking\n  description: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.\n  manifestation: I came in because my mind keeps going blank at the worst times, like I will look at the road-closure forms or the stop-slow paddle and suddenly cannot remember the steps I have done for years.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: I do not have the energy to fight the thought anymore that it might be better if I just did not wake up, and that scares my family because I have started saying it out loud.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and depressive_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: For many months, the voices and messages from trucks or radios have been happening during the same stretch when I wake up sad, guilty, barely eating, and wishing I would not wake up.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: depressive_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness (Criterion C)\n  manifestation: Looking back, most of this whole illness has been spent under that heavy depression, even during times when the voices or suspicious thoughts were quieter.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode (Criterion B)\n  manifestation: Before the depression got so deep, there were several weeks when I was still getting to work and church but already hearing my name and believing signs and radios were sending messages about me.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I stopped being reliable at work because I could not finish road-closure paperwork or trust myself with traffic-safety steps, and I pulled away from choir, volunteering, and family until they felt they had to bring me for help.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 8 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and eight months, starting after my closest friend died.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 1 year and 4 months\n  manifestation: The voices and the feeling that signs, trucks, or radios were sending messages about me have been happening for about a year and four months.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: The heavy depression, guilt, low energy, and loss of appetite have been with me for about a year and two months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 41 / crossing_guard_or_flagger / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S031\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I used to be the one doing things, you know? Like organizing rides for choir, and garden swaps—people would bring tomatoes and little pots of herbs, and someone always brought too much zucchini—and I’d help with snacks for the youth basketball games.\n\nNow I can sit for hours just staring at my phone. Messages come in and I see them, but I don’t feel that push to answer anybody. It’s like the part of me that used to care about all that is just… not there, or it’s very far away.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead or of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… yeah. Not like I’m sitting there with some big plan, but the thought comes, like maybe it would be better if I just didn’t wake up. And I used to push it away harder, or not say it, because saying things makes everyone look at you differently.\n\nBut lately I don’t have the energy to fight the thought the same way. I’ve started saying it out loud at home, like, “I wish I could just not wake up,” and then everybody gets scared and quiet, and then I feel bad for scaring them. It’s not… I don’t know. It feels awful to even say here.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time longing for your friend or feeling preoccupied with her death?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that exactly. Sometimes something reminds me of her, like a song in the grocery store or those little seasonal decorations they put out way too early, and then my mind kind of goes there. But I don’t know if I’d say I spend a lot of time on it. Time has been strange lately anyway.   },

I don’t… I don’t know if it’s like watching myself from the outside. That sounds very clear, like you’d be up in the corner of the room looking down, and I don’t think I’ve noticed that exactly.

Or I’ll realize the light changed outside and I haven’t moved. But I don’t know that I feel outside my body. I’m just… not really there with things, if that makes sense.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F43.2, F44.9, F41.1, F06.7\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s nice to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because my mind keeps… blanking out, I guess. Like at work, I’ll be looking right at the road-closure forms or holding the stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. It’s embarrassing, because that job is all about paying attention, and I used to know the routine like making coffee—well, I don’t even make it the same way every day, my sister says I put too much creamer in it—but with the road stuff, it’s serious. I just freeze and can’t think.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something very upsetting or frightening happened to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it did. I mean, I think that’s when it really started—after my closest friend died. That was about… a year and eight months ago now, which is strange because I still count it by months sometimes, like I’m keeping track of something. We used to talk about the dumbest things, grocery store music and whether birds know when it’s raining before people do, and then after she was gone, everything kind of… shifted. So, yes, after that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times when you couldn’t remember important personal information about yourself, not just the steps of a task?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not like my name or where I live or anything like that, if that’s what you mean. I mean, sometimes I’ll walk into a room and forget why I’m there, but my sister says everybody does that, especially if you’re carrying laundry and thinking about the grocery list at the same time.\n\nImportant personal information… I guess I’m not sure what counts. I haven’t noticed big pieces of myself missing, no. It’s more like I get stuck on what I’m supposed to be doing right then.\n\nDoctor: When your mind goes blank at work, do you feel intensely worried right then?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure if it’s worry exactly. I mean, I don’t stand there thinking, “I’m worried,” it’s more like everything gets… quiet and jammed up. Then afterward I think about it a lot, like what if I missed something, or if someone noticed.\n\nBut right in the moment? I don’t know. It’s hard to separate, because the cars are there and the signs, and sometimes there’s that smell from the asphalt when it’s hot, and my gloves never fit right. So I know something is happening, but I don’t know if “intensely worried” is the right word.\n\nDoctor: When your mind goes blank, does the world around you ever feel unreal or dreamlike?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… dreamlike is a hard word for me. Sometimes when I’m standing there it feels like I’m not catching up with what’s happening, like the cars and people are moving and I’m a second behind, but I don’t know if that means unreal.\n\nIt’s not like in movies where everything goes wavy or anything. I mostly notice little things, like the pole feeling too heavy or the beeping from a truck backing up, and then I’m thinking about how those beeps sound like the microwave my mom had when I was a kid, which is… not helpful when I’m supposed to be watching traffic. So, I guess I haven’t really thought of it as the world feeling dreamlike.\n\nDoctor: When your mind goes blank, do memories or images of your friend’s death suddenly come into your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not exactly like that. I mean, I know what you’re asking, I think, like if a picture of it just pops in, but when my mind goes blank it’s more like there’s nothing there for a second.\n\nSometimes a random thing will remind me of other random things, like a sound or the way the light hits the road, and then I’m off thinking about something else. Like yesterday there was this crow sitting on the sign, and I started thinking about how smart crows are and how my neighbor used to feed them peanuts. But I haven’t really noticed memories or images of my friend’s death coming in right then, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. Especially in the mornings. I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the day is already wrecked before I’ve even gotten out of bed.\n\nIt’s strange because the room can look the same, the curtains and the little crack in the ceiling I keep meaning to fix, and I’ll think about needing to buy spackle, and then I’m just lying there feeling like… what’s the point of starting. So yes, down. Depressed, I guess, most mornings and a lot of days.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed like this nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely longer than two weeks. It’s been, I don’t know, about a year and two months now, I think. That heavy depression feeling.\n\nIt kind of settled in and just… stayed. Like the low energy and the guilt, and not really wanting to eat much. I remember thinking last winter that maybe once the weather changed I’d feel different, because people always say sunlight helps, but then spring came and the trees were doing that bright green thing and I still felt the same. So… yes. Nearly every day, for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I used to be the one doing things, you know? Like organizing rides for choir, and garden swaps—people would bring tomatoes and little pots of herbs, and someone always brought too much zucchini—and I’d help with snacks for the youth basketball games.\n\nNow I can sit for hours just staring at my phone. Messages come in and I see them, but I don’t feel that push to answer anybody. It’s like the part of me that used to care about all that is just… not there, or it’s very far away.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead or of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… yeah. Not like I’m sitting there with some big plan, but the thought comes, like maybe it would be better if I just didn’t wake up. I’ve started saying it out loud at home, like, “I wish I could just not wake up,” and then everybody gets scared and quiet, and then I feel bad for scaring them. It’s not… I don’t know. It feels awful to even say here.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time longing for your friend or feeling preoccupied with her death?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that exactly. No… I don’t think it was only when I was this low. Before it got really deep, I was still doing things, still getting to work, still going to church — I mean, I’d put on the vest and go stand out there like normal, and people probably thought I was fine.

Sometimes when I’m standing there it feels like I’m not catching up with what’s happening, like the cars and people are moving and I’m a second behind, but I don’t know if that means unreal.\n\nIt’s not like in movies where everything goes wavy or anything. I mostly notice little things, like the pole feeling too heavy or the beeping from a truck backing up, and then I’m thinking about how those beeps sound like the microwave my mom had when I was a kid, which is… not helpful when I’m supposed to be watching traffic. Like yesterday there was this crow sitting on the sign, and I started thinking about how smart crows are and how my neighbor used to feed them peanuts. Like organizing rides for choir, and garden swaps—people would bring tomatoes and little pots of herbs, and someone always brought too much zucchini—and I’d help with snacks for the youth basketball games.\n\nNow I can sit for hours just staring at my phone. Messages come in and I see them, but I don’t feel that push to answer anybody. It’s like the part of me that used to care about all that is just… not there, or it’s very far away.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead or of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… yeah. So I know something is happening, but I don’t know if “intensely worried” is the right word.\n\nDoctor: When your mind goes blank, does the world around you ever feel unreal or dreamlike?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… dreamlike is a hard word for me. Sometimes when I’m standing there it feels like I’m not catching up with what’s happening, like the cars and people are moving and I’m a second behind, but I don’t know if that means unreal.\n\nIt’s not like in movies where everything goes wavy or anything. I mostly notice little things, like the pole feeling too heavy or the beeping from a truck backing up, and then I’m thinking about how those beeps sound like the microwave my mom had when I was a kid, which is… not helpful when I’m supposed to be watching traffic. So, I guess I haven’t really thought of it as the world feeling dreamlike.\n\nDoctor: When your mind goes blank, do memories or images of your friend’s death suddenly come into your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not exactly like that. I mean, I know what you’re asking, I think, like if a picture of it just pops in, but when my mind goes blank it’s more like there’s nothing there for a second.\n\nSometimes a random thing will remind me of other random things, like a sound or the way the light hits the road, and then I’m off thinking about something else. Like yesterday there was this crow sitting on the sign, and I started thinking about how smart crows are and how my neighbor used to feed them peanuts. But I haven’t really noticed memories or images of my friend’s death coming in right then, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. Especially in the mornings. I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the day is already wrecked before I’ve even gotten out of bed.\n\nIt’s strange because the room can look the same, the curtains and the little crack in the ceiling I keep meaning to fix, and I’ll think about needing to buy spackle, and then I’m just lying there feeling like… what’s the point of starting. So yes, down. Depressed, I guess, most mornings and a lot of days.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed like this nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely longer than two weeks. It’s been, I don’t know, about a year and two months now, I think. That heavy depression feeling.\n\nIt kind of settled in and just… stayed. Like the low energy and the guilt, and not really wanting to eat much. I remember thinking last winter that maybe once the weather changed I’d feel different, because people always say sunlight helps, but then spring came and the trees were doing that bright green thing and I still felt the same. So… yes. Nearly every day, for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I used to be the one doing things, you know? Like organizing rides for choir, and garden swaps—people would bring tomatoes and little pots of herbs, and someone always brought too much zucchini—and I’d help with snacks for the youth basketball games.\n\nNow I can sit for hours just staring at my phone. Messages come in and I see them, but I don’t feel that push to answer anybody. It’s like the part of me that used to care about all that is just… not there, or it’s very far away.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead or of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… yeah. Not like I’m sitting there with some big plan, but the thought comes, like maybe it would be better if I just didn’t wake up. And I used to push it away harder, or not say it, because saying things makes everyone look at you differently.\n\nBut lately I don’t have the energy to fight the thought the same way. I’ve started saying it out loud at home, like, “I wish I could just not wake up,” and then everybody gets scared and quiet, and then I feel bad for scaring them. It’s not… I don’t know. It feels awful to even say here.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time longing for your friend or feeling preoccupied with her death?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I think about people who’ve passed, sure, but “longing” sounds so… specific, like something you’d read on one of those pamphlets they put in the waiting room.\n\nWith my friend, I don’t know. Sometimes something reminds me of her, like a song in the grocery store or those little seasonal decorations they put out way too early, and then my mind kind of goes there. But I don’t know if I’d say I spend a lot of time on it. Time has been strange lately anyway. Some days disappear and I couldn’t tell you what I was thinking about most of the time.\n\nDoctor: During those blank moments, do you feel detached from yourself, like you’re watching yourself from the outside?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t know if it’s like watching myself from the outside. That sounds very clear, like you’d be up in the corner of the room looking down, and I don’t think I’ve noticed that exactly.\n\nIt’s more like I lose track of what I was doing. Like I’ll be holding my phone, and then the screen’s gone dark, and I’m still just sitting there. Or I’ll realize the light changed outside and I haven’t moved. But I don’t know that I feel outside my body. I’m just… not really there with things, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: When your mind goes blank, do those episodes happen only when your mood is very low?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it was only when I was this low. Before it got really deep, I was still doing things, still getting to work, still going to church — I mean, I’d put on the vest and go stand out there like normal, and people probably thought I was fine.\n\nBut even then, for several weeks, things were already… off. I’d hear my name sometimes, and I kept thinking signs or the radio were saying things about me. Like not just a normal coincidence. And then I’d try to act regular because you don’t want people at church looking over like, “What’s wrong with her?” So… no, it wasn’t only when my mood was at the bottom.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if the radio was saying “punishment” exactly, not like a judge reading it out loud. But it felt like… like it was pointing at me. Like I had done something wrong and everybody else somehow knew.

And then my mind goes to everything I didn’t do right. With my friend, especially. I keep replaying little things—calls I should’ve made, times I should’ve noticed something, or been better somehow. And then it spreads, you know? Like the choir, my family… even small things, like forgetting something someone asked me for, it all stacks up.

I start feeling like I’m just useless. Like I failed everybody, and if something bad is coming to me, maybe I deserve it. Which sounds awful to say out loud. But that’s where my head goes.

I… yes. I mean, not in a clear way like I think someone should come and do something to me, but inside it feels like I must have earned it somehow.

I keep going over every little thing I didn’t do right — with my friend, mostly, but then it turns into the choir, my family, all of it. Like I should’ve called, or noticed, or helped more, or just been… better. And then it gets so tangled up that I feel like I’m just useless.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because my mind keeps… blanking out, I guess. It’s embarrassing, because that job is all about paying attention, and I used to know the routine like making coffee—well, I don’t even make it the same way every day, my sister says I put too much creamer in it—but with the road stuff, it’s serious. I just freeze and can’t think.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something very upsetting or frightening happened to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it did. I mean, I think that’s when it really started—after my closest friend died. That was about… a year and eight months ago now, which is strange because I still count it by months sometimes, like I’m keeping track of something. We used to talk about the dumbest things, grocery store music and whether birds know when it’s raining before people do, and then after she was gone, everything kind of… shifted. So, yes, after that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times when you couldn’t remember important personal information about yourself, not just the steps of a task?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not like my name or where I live or anything like that, if that’s what you mean. I mean, sometimes I’ll walk into a room and forget why I’m there, but my sister says everybody does that, especially if you’re carrying laundry and thinking about the grocery list at the same time.\n\nImportant personal information… I guess I’m not sure what counts. I haven’t noticed big pieces of myself missing, no. It’s more like I get stuck on what I’m supposed to be doing right then.\n\nDoctor: When your mind goes blank at work, do you feel intensely worried right then?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure if it’s worry exactly. I mean, I don’t stand there thinking, “I’m worried,” it’s more like everything gets… quiet and jammed up. Then afterward I think about it a lot, like what if I missed something, or if someone noticed.\n\nBut right in the moment? I don’t know. It’s hard to separate, because the cars are there and the signs, and sometimes there’s that smell from the asphalt when it’s hot, and my gloves never fit right. So I know something is happening, but I don’t know if “intensely worried” is the right word.\n\nDoctor: When your mind goes blank, does the world around you ever feel unreal or dreamlike?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… dreamlike is a hard word for me. Sometimes when I’m standing there it feels like I’m not catching up with what’s happening, like the cars and people are moving and I’m a second behind, but I don’t know if that means unreal.\n\nIt’s not like in movies where everything goes wavy or anything. I mostly notice little things, like the pole feeling too heavy or the beeping from a truck backing up, and then I’m thinking about how those beeps sound like the microwave my mom had when I was a kid, which is… not helpful when I’m supposed to be watching traffic. So, I guess I haven’t really thought of it as the world feeling dreamlike.\n\nDoctor: When your mind goes blank, do memories or images of your friend’s death suddenly come into your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not exactly like that. I mean, I know what you’re asking, I think, like if a picture of it just pops in, but when my mind goes blank it’s more like there’s nothing there for a second.\n\nSometimes a random thing will remind me of other random things, like a sound or the way the light hits the road, and then I’m off thinking about something else. Like yesterday there was this crow sitting on the sign, and I started thinking about how smart crows are and how my neighbor used to feed them peanuts. But I haven’t really noticed memories or images of my friend’s death coming in right then, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. Especially in the mornings. I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the day is already wrecked before I’ve even gotten out of bed.\n\nIt’s strange because the room can look the same, the curtains and the little crack in the ceiling I keep meaning to fix, and I’ll think about needing to buy spackle, and then I’m just lying there feeling like… what’s the point of starting. So yes, down. Depressed, I guess, most mornings and a lot of days.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed like this nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely longer than two weeks. It’s been, I don’t know, about a year and two months now, I think. That heavy depression feeling.\n\nIt kind of settled in and just… stayed. Like the low energy and the guilt, and not really wanting to eat much. I remember thinking last winter that maybe once the weather changed I’d feel different, because people always say sunlight helps, but then spring came and the trees were doing that bright green thing and I still felt the same. So… yes. Nearly every day, for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I used to be the one doing things, you know? Like organizing rides for choir, and garden swaps—people would bring tomatoes and little pots of herbs, and someone always brought too much zucchini—and I’d help with snacks for the youth basketball games.\n\nNow I can sit for hours just staring at my phone. Messages come in and I see them, but I don’t feel that push to answer anybody. It’s like the part of me that used to care about all that is just… not there, or it’s very far away.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead or of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… yeah. Not like I’m sitting there with some big plan, but the thought comes, like maybe it would be better if I just didn’t wake up. And I used to push it away harder, or not say it, because saying things makes everyone look at you differently.\n\nBut lately I don’t have the energy to fight the thought the same way. I’ve started saying it out loud at home, like, “I wish I could just not wake up,” and then everybody gets scared and quiet, and then I feel bad for scaring them. It’s not… I don’t know. It feels awful to even say here.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time longing for your friend or feeling preoccupied with her death?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I think about people who’ve passed, sure, but “longing” sounds so… specific, like something you’d read on one of those pamphlets they put in the waiting room.\n\nWith my friend, I don’t know. Sometimes something reminds me of her, like a song in the grocery store or those little seasonal decorations they put out way too early, and then my mind kind of goes there. But I don’t know if I’d say I spend a lot of time on it. Time has been strange lately anyway. Some days disappear and I couldn’t tell you what I was thinking about most of the time.\n\nDoctor: During those blank moments, do you feel detached from yourself, like you’re watching yourself from the outside?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t know if it’s like watching myself from the outside. That sounds very clear, like you’d be up in the corner of the room looking down, and I don’t think I’ve noticed that exactly.\n\nIt’s more like I lose track of what I was doing. Like I’ll be holding my phone, and then the screen’s gone dark, and I’m still just sitting there. Or I’ll realize the light changed outside and I haven’t moved. But I don’t know that I feel outside my body. I’m just… not really there with things, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: When your mind goes blank, do those episodes happen only when your mood is very low?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it was only when I was this low. Before it got really deep, I was still doing things, still getting to work, still going to church — I mean, I’d put on the vest and go stand out there like normal, and people probably thought I was fine.\n\nBut even then, for several weeks, things were already… off. I’d hear my name sometimes, and I kept thinking signs or the radio were saying things about me. Like not just a normal coincidence. And then I’d try to act regular because you don’t want people at church looking over like, “What’s wrong with her?” So… no, it wasn’t only when my mood was at the bottom.\n\nDoctor: When you heard your name or thought signs or the radio were saying things about you, did you believe those messages meant you had done something wrong or should be punished?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if the radio was saying “punishment” exactly, not like a judge reading it out loud. But it felt like… like it was pointing at me. Like I had done something wrong and everybody else somehow knew.\n\nAnd then my mind goes to everything I didn’t do right. With my friend, especially. I keep replaying little things—calls I should’ve made, times I should’ve noticed something, or been better somehow. And then it spreads, you know? Like the choir, my family… even small things, like forgetting something someone asked me for, it all stacks up.\n\nI start feeling like I’m just useless. Like I failed everybody, and if something bad is coming to me, maybe I deserve it. Which sounds awful to say out loud. But that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you deserve to be punished?\n\nPatient: I… yes. I mean, not in a clear way like I think someone should come and do something to me, but inside it feels like I must have earned it somehow.\n\nI keep going over every little thing I didn’t do right — with my friend, mostly, but then it turns into the choir, my family, all of it. Like I should’ve called, or noticed, or helped more, or just been… better. And then it gets so tangled up that I feel like I’m just useless.\n\nSo when I think something bad is coming, part of me thinks… maybe that’s what I deserve.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Bereavement-related adjustment remains possible but less likely given duration/severity with psychotic features. Organic mild cognitive disorder not excluded yet.","is_final":false}

If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 16\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
{"key":"S019","name":"Delusions","description":"Fixed beliefs that do not change easily, even in the face of contradictory evidence.","reason":"The doctor is asking about a fixed belief that signs or the radio were sending messages specifically meant for the patient, which directly reflects delusional ideas of reference."}

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Schizoaffective Disorder (Depressive Type), in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: When your mind goes blank, do those episodes happen only when your mood is very low?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it was only when I was this low. Before it got really deep, I was still doing things, still getting to work, still going to church — I mean, I’d put on the vest and go stand out there like normal, and people probably thought I was fine.\n\nBut even then, for several weeks, things were already… off. I’d hear my name sometimes, and I kept thinking signs or the radio were saying things about me. Like not just a normal coincidence. And then I’d try to act regular because you don’t want people at church looking over like, “What’s wrong with her?” So… no, it wasn’t only when my mood was at the bottom.\n\nDoctor: When you heard your name or thought signs or the radio were saying things about you, did you believe those messages meant you had done something wrong or should be punished?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if the radio was saying “punishment” exactly, not like a judge reading it out loud. But it felt like… like it was pointing at me. Like I had done something wrong and everybody else somehow knew.\n\nAnd then my mind goes to everything I didn’t do right. With my friend, especially. I keep replaying little things—calls I should’ve made, times I should’ve noticed something, or been better somehow. And then it spreads, you know? Like the choir, my family… even small things, like forgetting something someone asked me for, it all stacks up.\n\nI start feeling like I’m just useless. Like I failed everybody, and if something bad is coming to me, maybe I deserve it. Which sounds awful to say out loud. But that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you deserve to be punished?\n\nPatient: I… yes. I mean, not in a clear way like I think someone should come and do something to me, but inside it feels like I must have earned it somehow.\n\nI keep going over every little thing I didn’t do right — with my friend, mostly, but then it turns into the choir, my family, all of it. Like I should’ve called, or noticed, or helped more, or just been… better. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I do. I know my sister says it’s not about me, she’ll say, “That’s just a road sign,” or “People are just talking,” but it doesn’t feel that way.

Like the city trucks, when they pass by, or the police radios — I feel like they’re keeping tabs on me. And if one of those signs flashes while I’m near it, I know it’s meant for me somehow. Even at church, if somebody lowers their voice when I walk by, I can’t shake it. It feels like they know something and they’re saying it without saying it.

I know how that sounds… but yes, it feels specific to me.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.3, F32.9, F43.2, F44.9, F06.7\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s nice to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because my mind keeps… blanking out, I guess. Like at work, I’ll be looking right at the road-closure forms or holding the stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. It’s embarrassing, because that job is all about paying attention, and I used to know the routine like making coffee—well, I don’t even make it the same way every day, my sister says I put too much creamer in it—but with the road stuff, it’s serious. I just freeze and can’t think.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something very upsetting or frightening happened to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it did. I mean, I think that’s when it really started—after my closest friend died. That was about… a year and eight months ago now, which is strange because I still count it by months sometimes, like I’m keeping track of something. We used to talk about the dumbest things, grocery store music and whether birds know when it’s raining before people do, and then after she was gone, everything kind of… shifted. So, yes, after that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times when you couldn’t remember important personal information about yourself, not just the steps of a task?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not like my name or where I live or anything like that, if that’s what you mean. I mean, sometimes I’ll walk into a room and forget why I’m there, but my sister says everybody does that, especially if you’re carrying laundry and thinking about the grocery list at the same time.\n\nImportant personal information… I guess I’m not sure what counts. I haven’t noticed big pieces of myself missing, no. It’s more like I get stuck on what I’m supposed to be doing right then.\n\nDoctor: When your mind goes blank at work, do you feel intensely worried right then?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure if it’s worry exactly. I mean, I don’t stand there thinking, “I’m worried,” it’s more like everything gets… quiet and jammed up. Then afterward I think about it a lot, like what if I missed something, or if someone noticed.\n\nBut right in the moment? I don’t know. It’s hard to separate, because the cars are there and the signs, and sometimes there’s that smell from the asphalt when it’s hot, and my gloves never fit right. So I know something is happening, but I don’t know if “intensely worried” is the right word.\n\nDoctor: When your mind goes blank, does the world around you ever feel unreal or dreamlike?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… dreamlike is a hard word for me. Sometimes when I’m standing there it feels like I’m not catching up with what’s happening, like the cars and people are moving and I’m a second behind, but I don’t know if that means unreal.\n\nIt’s not like in movies where everything goes wavy or anything. I mostly notice little things, like the pole feeling too heavy or the beeping from a truck backing up, and then I’m thinking about how those beeps sound like the microwave my mom had when I was a kid, which is… not helpful when I’m supposed to be watching traffic. So, I guess I haven’t really thought of it as the world feeling dreamlike.\n\nDoctor: When your mind goes blank, do memories or images of your friend’s death suddenly come into your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not exactly like that. I mean, I know what you’re asking, I think, like if a picture of it just pops in, but when my mind goes blank it’s more like there’s nothing there for a second.\n\nSometimes a random thing will remind me of other random things, like a sound or the way the light hits the road, and then I’m off thinking about something else. Like yesterday there was this crow sitting on the sign, and I started thinking about how smart crows are and how my neighbor used to feed them peanuts. But I haven’t really noticed memories or images of my friend’s death coming in right then, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. Especially in the mornings. I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the day is already wrecked before I’ve even gotten out of bed.\n\nIt’s strange because the room can look the same, the curtains and the little crack in the ceiling I keep meaning to fix, and I’ll think about needing to buy spackle, and then I’m just lying there feeling like… what’s the point of starting. So yes, down. Depressed, I guess, most mornings and a lot of days.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed like this nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely longer than two weeks. It’s been, I don’t know, about a year and two months now, I think. That heavy depression feeling.\n\nIt kind of settled in and just… stayed. Like the low energy and the guilt, and not really wanting to eat much. I remember thinking last winter that maybe once the weather changed I’d feel different, because people always say sunlight helps, but then spring came and the trees were doing that bright green thing and I still felt the same. So… yes. Nearly every day, for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I used to be the one doing things, you know? Like organizing rides for choir, and garden swaps—people would bring tomatoes and little pots of herbs, and someone always brought too much zucchini—and I’d help with snacks for the youth basketball games.\n\nNow I can sit for hours just staring at my phone. Messages come in and I see them, but I don’t feel that push to answer anybody. It’s like the part of me that used to care about all that is just… not there, or it’s very far away.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead or of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… yeah. Not like I’m sitting there with some big plan, but the thought comes, like maybe it would be better if I just didn’t wake up. And I used to push it away harder, or not say it, because saying things makes everyone look at you differently.\n\nBut lately I don’t have the energy to fight the thought the same way. I’ve started saying it out loud at home, like, “I wish I could just not wake up,” and then everybody gets scared and quiet, and then I feel bad for scaring them. It’s not… I don’t know. It feels awful to even say here.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time longing for your friend or feeling preoccupied with her death?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I think about people who’ve passed, sure, but “longing” sounds so… specific, like something you’d read on one of those pamphlets they put in the waiting room.\n\nWith my friend, I don’t know. Sometimes something reminds me of her, like a song in the grocery store or those little seasonal decorations they put out way too early, and then my mind kind of goes there. But I don’t know if I’d say I spend a lot of time on it. Time has been strange lately anyway. Some days disappear and I couldn’t tell you what I was thinking about most of the time.\n\nDoctor: During those blank moments, do you feel detached from yourself, like you’re watching yourself from the outside?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t know if it’s like watching myself from the outside. That sounds very clear, like you’d be up in the corner of the room looking down, and I don’t think I’ve noticed that exactly.\n\nIt’s more like I lose track of what I was doing. Like I’ll be holding my phone, and then the screen’s gone dark, and I’m still just sitting there. Or I’ll realize the light changed outside and I haven’t moved. But I don’t know that I feel outside my body. I’m just… not really there with things, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: When your mind goes blank, do those episodes happen only when your mood is very low?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it was only when I was this low. Before it got really deep, I was still doing things, still getting to work, still going to church — I mean, I’d put on the vest and go stand out there like normal, and people probably thought I was fine.\n\nBut even then, for several weeks, things were already… off. I’d hear my name sometimes, and I kept thinking signs or the radio were saying things about me. Like not just a normal coincidence. And then I’d try to act regular because you don’t want people at church looking over like, “What’s wrong with her?” So… no, it wasn’t only when my mood was at the bottom.\n\nDoctor: When you heard your name or thought signs or the radio were saying things about you, did you believe those messages meant you had done something wrong or should be punished?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if the radio was saying “punishment” exactly, not like a judge reading it out loud. Like I failed everybody, and if something bad is coming to me, maybe I deserve it. Which sounds awful to say out loud. But that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you deserve to be punished?\n\nPatient: I… yes. And then I’d try to act regular because you don’t want people at church looking over like, “What’s wrong with her?” So… no, it wasn’t only when my mood was at the bottom.\n\nDoctor: When you heard your name or thought signs or the radio were saying things about you, did you believe those messages meant you had done something wrong or should be punished?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if the radio was saying “punishment” exactly, not like a judge reading it out loud. But it felt like… like it was pointing at me. Like I had done something wrong and everybody else somehow knew.\n\nAnd then my mind goes to everything I didn’t do right. With my friend, especially. I keep replaying little things—calls I should’ve made, times I should’ve noticed something, or been better somehow. And then it spreads, you know? Like the choir, my family… even small things, like forgetting something someone asked me for, it all stacks up.\n\nI start feeling like I’m just useless. Like I failed everybody, and if something bad is coming to me, maybe I deserve it. Which sounds awful to say out loud. But that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you deserve to be punished?\n\nPatient: I… yes. I mean, not in a clear way like I think someone should come and do something to me, but inside it feels like I must have earned it somehow.\n\nI keep going over every little thing I didn’t do right — with my friend, mostly, but then it turns into the choir, my family, all of it. Like I should’ve called, or noticed, or helped more, or just been… better. And then it gets so tangled up that I feel like I’m just useless.\n\nSo when I think something bad is coming, part of me thinks… maybe that’s what I deserve.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe those signs or the radio were truly sending messages meant specifically for you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know my sister says it’s not about me, she’ll say, “That’s just a road sign,” or “People are just talking,” but it doesn’t feel that way.\n\nLike the city trucks, when they pass by, or the police radios — I feel like they’re keeping tabs on me. And if one of those signs flashes while I’m near it, I know it’s meant for me somehow. Even at church, if somebody lowers their voice when I walk by, I can’t shake it. It feels like they know something and they’re saying it without saying it.\n\nI know how that sounds… but yes, it feels specific to me."
